An experiment examined the extent to which individuals could refute arguments that contradicted their personal beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-albarracin-25

People with high

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence

levels more readily seek out contradictory information to their personal position to form an argument. This can take the form of an

oppositional news consumption

, where individuals seek opposing partisan news in order to counterargue.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-27

Individuals with low confidence levels do not seek out contradictory information and prefer information that supports their personal position. People generate and evaluate evidence in arguments that are biased towards their own beliefs and opinions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-stanovich-28

Heightened confidence levels decrease preference for information that supports individuals' personal beliefs.

Another experiment gave participants a complex rule-discovery task that involved moving objects simulated by a computer.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-mynatt1978-29

Objects on the computer screen followed specific laws, which the participants had to figure out. So, participants could "fire" objects across the screen to test their hypotheses. Despite making many attempts over a ten-hour session, none of the participants figured out the rules of the system. They typically attempted to confirm rather than falsify their hypotheses, and were reluctant to consider alternatives. Even after seeing objective evidence that refuted their working hypotheses, they frequently continued doing the same tests. Some of the participants were taught proper hypothesis-testing, but these instructions had almost no effect.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-mynatt1978-29

Biased interpretation of information

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=4

Smart people believe weird things because they are skilled at defending beliefs they arrived at for non-smart reasons.

Michael Shermer

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael\_Shermer

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-30

Confirmation biases are not limited to the collection of evidence. Even if two individuals have the same information, the way they interpret it can be biased.

Stanford University

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford\_University

conducted an experiment involving participants who felt strongly about capital punishment, with half in favor and half against it.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron201-32

Each participant read descriptions of two studies: a comparison of

U.S. states

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.\_state

with and without the death penalty, and a comparison of murder rates in a state before and after the introduction of the death penalty. After reading a quick description of each study, the participants were asked whether their opinions had changed. Then, they read a more detailed account of each study's procedure and had to rate whether the research was well-conducted and convincing.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

In fact, the studies were fictional. Half the participants were told that one kind of study supported the

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deterrence\_(psychology)

effect and the other undermined it, while for other participants the conclusions were swapped.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron201-32

The participants, whether supporters or opponents, reported shifting their attitudes slightly in the direction of the first study they read. Once they read the more detailed descriptions of the two studies, they almost all returned to their original belief regardless of the evidence provided, pointing to details that supported their viewpoint and disregarding anything contrary. Participants described studies supporting their pre-existing view as superior to those that contradicted it, in detailed and specific ways.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-vyse122-33

Writing about a study that seemed to undermine the deterrence effect, a death penalty proponent wrote, "The research didn't cover a long enough period of time," while an opponent's comment on the same study said, "No strong evidence to contradict the researchers has been presented."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

The results illustrated that people set higher standards of evidence for hypotheses that go against their current expectations. This effect, known as "disconfirmation bias", has been supported by other experiments.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-taber\_political-34

Another study of biased interpretation occurred during the

2004 U.S. presidential election

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004\_United\_States\_presidential\_election

and involved participants who reported having strong feelings about the candidates. They were shown apparently contradictory pairs of statements, either from Republican candidate

George W. Bush

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George\_W.\_Bush

, Democratic candidate

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John\_Kerry

or a politically neutral public figure. They were also given further statements that made the apparent contradiction seem reasonable. From these three pieces of information, they had to decide whether each individual's statements were inconsistent.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-westen2006-35

There were strong differences in these evaluations, with participants much more likely to interpret statements from the candidate they opposed as contradictory.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-westen2006-35

MRI scanner

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic\_resonance\_imaging

allowed researchers to examine how the human brain deals with dissonant information.

In this experiment, the participants made their judgments while in a

magnetic resonance imaging

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic\_resonance\_imaging

(MRI) scanner which monitored their brain activity. As participants evaluated contradictory statements by their favored candidate,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotion

centers of their brains were aroused. This did not happen with the statements by the other figures. The experimenters inferred that the different responses to the statements were not due to passive reasoning errors. Instead, the participants were actively reducing the

cognitive dissonance

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_dissonance

induced by reading about their favored candidate's irrational or

hypocritical

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypocrisy

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-westen2006-35

Biases in belief interpretation are persistent, regardless of intelligence level. Participants in an experiment took the

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAT

test (a college admissions test used in the United States) to assess their intelligence levels. They then read information regarding safety concerns for vehicles, and the experimenters manipulated the national origin of the car. American participants provided their opinion if the car should be banned on a six-point scale, where one indicated "definitely yes" and six indicated "definitely no". Participants firstly evaluated if they would allow a dangerous German car on American streets and a dangerous American car on German streets. Participants believed that the dangerous German car on American streets should be banned more quickly than the dangerous American car on German streets. There was no difference among intelligence levels at the rate participants would ban a car.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-stanovich-28

Biased interpretation is not restricted to emotionally significant topics. In another experiment, participants were told a story about a theft. They had to rate the evidential importance of statements arguing either for or against a particular character being responsible. When they hypothesized that character's guilt, they rated statements supporting that hypothesis as more important than conflicting statements.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-36

Biased recall of information

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=5

People may remember evidence selectively to reinforce their expectations, even if they gather and interpret evidence in a neutral manner. This effect is called "selective recall", "confirmatory memory", or "access-biased memory".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-37

Psychological theories differ in their predictions about selective recall.

Schema theory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schema\_(psychology)

predicts that information matching prior expectations will be more easily stored and recalled than information that does not match.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-oswald88-38

Some alternative approaches say that surprising information stands out and so is memorable.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-oswald88-38

Predictions from both these theories have been confirmed in different experimental contexts, with no theory winning outright.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-39

In one study, participants read a profile of a woman which described a mix of introverted and extroverted behaviors.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-snydercantor-40

They later had to recall examples of her introversion and extroversion. One group was told this was to assess the woman for a job as a librarian, while a second group were told it was for a job in real estate sales. There was a significant difference between what these two groups recalled, with the "librarian" group recalling more examples of introversion and the "sales" groups recalling more extroverted behavior.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-snydercantor-40

A selective memory effect has also been shown in experiments that manipulate the desirability of personality types.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-oswald88-38

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-41

In one of these, a group of participants were shown evidence that extroverted people are more successful than introverts. Another group were told the opposite. In a subsequent, apparently unrelated study, participants were asked to recall events from their lives in which they had been either introverted or extroverted. Each group of participants provided more memories connecting themselves with the more desirable personality type, and recalled those memories more quickly.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-42

Changes in emotional states can also influence memory recall.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-levine-43

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-safer-44

Participants rated how they felt when they had first learned that

O. J. Simpson

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O.\_J.\_Simpson

had been acquitted of murder charges.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-levine-43

They described their emotional reactions and confidence regarding the verdict one week, two months, and one year after the trial. Results indicated that participants' assessments for Simpson's guilt changed over time. The more that participants' opinion of the verdict had changed, the less stable were the participant's memories regarding their initial emotional reactions. When participants recalled their initial emotional reactions two months and a year later, past appraisals closely resembled current appraisals of emotion. People demonstrate sizable myside bias when discussing their opinions on controversial topics.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-stanovich-28

Memory recall and construction of experiences undergo revision in relation to corresponding emotional states.

Myside bias has been shown to influence the accuracy of memory recall.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-safer-44

In an experiment, widows and widowers rated the intensity of their experienced grief six months and five years after the deaths of their spouses. Participants noted a higher experience of grief at six months rather than at five years. Yet, when the participants were asked after five years how they had felt six months after the death of their significant other, the intensity of grief participants recalled was highly

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correlation

with their current level of grief. Individuals appear to utilize their current emotional states to analyze how they must have felt when experiencing past events.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-levine-43

Emotional memories are reconstructed by current emotional states.

One study showed how selective memory can maintain belief in

extrasensory perception

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrasensory\_perception

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-russell\_jones-45

Believers and disbelievers were each shown descriptions of ESP experiments. Half of each group were told that the experimental results supported the existence of ESP, while the others were told they did not. In a subsequent test, participants recalled the material accurately, apart from believers who had read the non-supportive evidence. This group remembered significantly less information and some of them incorrectly remembered the results as supporting ESP.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-russell\_jones-45

Individual differences

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=6

Myside bias was once believed to be correlated with intelligence; however, studies have shown that myside bias can be more influenced by ability to rationally think as opposed to level of intelligence.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-stanovich-28

Myside bias can cause an inability to effectively and logically evaluate the opposite side of an argument. Studies have stated that myside bias is an absence of "active open-mindedness", meaning the active search for why an initial idea may be wrong.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Baron\_1995-46

Typically, myside bias is operationalized in empirical studies as the quantity of evidence used in support of their side in comparison to the opposite side.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Wolfe\_2008-47

A study has found individual differences in myside bias. This study investigates individual differences that are acquired through learning in a cultural context and are mutable. The researcher found important individual difference in argumentation. Studies have suggested that individual differences such as deductive reasoning ability, ability to overcome belief bias, epistemological understanding, and thinking disposition are significant predictors of the reasoning and generating arguments, counterarguments, and rebuttals.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Mason\_2006-48

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Weinstock\_2009-49

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Weinstock\_2004-50

A study by Christopher Wolfe and Anne Britt also investigated how participants' views of "what makes a good argument?" can be a source of myside bias that influences the way a person formulates their own arguments.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Wolfe\_2008-47

The study investigated individual differences of argumentation schema and asked participants to write essays. The participants were randomly assigned to write essays either for or against their preferred side of an argument and were given research instructions that took either a balanced or an unrestricted approach. The balanced-research instructions directed participants to create a "balanced" argument, i.e., that included both pros and cons; the unrestricted-research instructions included nothing on how to create the argument.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Wolfe\_2008-47

Overall, the results revealed that the balanced-research instructions significantly increased the incidence of opposing information in arguments. These data also reveal that personal belief is not a

of myside bias; however, that those participants, who believe that a good argument is one that is based on facts, are more likely to exhibit myside bias than other participants. This evidence is consistent with the claims proposed in Baron's article—that people's opinions about what makes good thinking can influence how arguments are generated.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Wolfe\_2008-47

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=7

Informal observations

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=8

Francis Bacon

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis\_Bacon

Before psychological research on confirmation bias, the phenomenon had been observed throughout history. Beginning with the Greek historian

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thucydides

( c. 460 BC – c. 395 BC), who wrote of misguided reason in

The Peloponnesian War

; "... for it is a habit of mankind to entrust to careless hope what they long for, and to use sovereign reason to thrust aside what they do not fancy".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-51

Italian poet

Dante Alighieri

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante\_Alighieri

(1265–1321) noted it in the

Divine Comedy

St. Thomas Aquinas

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St.\_Thomas\_Aquinas

cautions Dante upon meeting in Paradise, "opinion—hasty—often can incline to the wrong side, and then affection for one's own opinion binds, confines the mind".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-52

Ibn Khaldun

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn\_Khaldun

noticed the same effect in his

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-53

Untruth naturally afflicts historical information. There are various reasons that make this unavoidable. One of them is partisanship for opinions and schools.... if the soul is infected with partisanship for a particular opinion or sect, it accepts without a moment's hesitation the information that is agreeable to it. Prejudice and partisanship obscure the critical faculty and preclude critical investigation. The result is that falsehoods are accepted and transmitted.

Novum Organum

, English philosopher and scientist

Francis Bacon

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis\_Bacon

(1561–1626)

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron195-54

noted that biased assessment of evidence drove "all superstitions, whether in astrology, dreams, omens, divine judgments or the like".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-bacon-55

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-bacon-55

The human understanding when it has once adopted an opinion ... draws all things else to support and agree with it. And though there be a greater number and weight of instances to be found on the other side, yet these it either neglects or despises, or else by some distinction sets aside or rejects\[.\]

In the second volume of his

The World as Will and Representation

(1844), German philosopher

Arthur Schopenhauer

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur\_Schopenhauer

observed that "An adopted hypothesis gives us lynx-eyes for everything that confirms it and makes us blind to everything that contradicts it."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-56

In his essay (1897)

What Is Art?

, Russian novelist

Leo Tolstoy

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo\_Tolstoy

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-:1-57

I know that most men—not only those considered clever, but even those who are very clever, and capable of understanding most difficult scientific, mathematical, or philosophic problems—can very seldom discern even the simplest and most obvious truth if it be such as to oblige them to admit the falsity of conclusions they have formed, perhaps with much difficulty—conclusions of which they are proud, which they have taught to others, and on which they have built their lives.

In his essay (1894)

The Kingdom of God Is Within You

, Tolstoy had earlier written:

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-:2-58

The most difficult subjects can be explained to the most slow-witted man if he has not formed any idea of them already; but the simplest thing cannot be made clear to the most intelligent man if he is firmly persuaded that he knows already, without a shadow of doubt, what is laid before him.

Hypothesis-testing (falsification) explanation (Wason)

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=9

#### Main article:

Wason selection task

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wason\_selection\_task

In Peter Wason's initial experiment published in 1960 (which does not mention the term "confirmation bias"), he repeatedly challenged participants to identify a rule applying to triples of numbers. They were told that (2,4,6) fits the rule. They generated triples, and the experimenter told them whether each triple conformed to the rule.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

The actual rule was simply "any ascending sequence", but participants had great difficulty in finding it, often announcing rules that were far more specific, such as "the middle number is the average of the first and last".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-59

The participants seemed to test only positive examples—triples that obeyed their hypothesized rule. For example, if they thought the rule was, "Each number is two greater than its predecessor," they would offer a triple that fitted (confirmed) this rule, such as (11,13,15) rather than a triple that violated (falsified) it, such as (11,12,19).

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-60

Wason interpreted his results as showing a preference for confirmation over falsification, hence he coined the term "confirmation bias".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-62

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-oswald-63

Wason also used confirmation bias to explain the results of his

selection task

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wason\_selection\_task

experiment.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-64

Participants repeatedly performed badly on various forms of this test, in most cases ignoring information that could potentially refute (falsify) the specified rule.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-sutherland-65

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-66

Hypothesis testing (positive test strategy) explanation (Klayman and Ha)

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=10

Klayman and Ha's 1987 paper argues that the Wason experiments do not actually demonstrate a bias towards confirmation, but instead a tendency to make tests consistent with the working hypothesis.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-klaymanha-20

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-67

They called this the "positive test strategy".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kunda112-15

This strategy is an example of a

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristics\_in\_judgment\_and\_decision\_making

: a reasoning shortcut that is imperfect but easy to compute.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-plous233-68

Klayman and Ha used

Bayesian probability

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayesian\_probability

information theory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information\_theory

as their standard of hypothesis-testing, rather than the falsificationism used by Wason. According to these ideas, each answer to a question yields a different amount of information, which depends on the person's prior beliefs. Thus a scientific test of a hypothesis is one that is expected to produce the most information. Since the information content depends on initial probabilities, a positive test can either be highly informative or uninformative. Klayman and Ha argued that when people think about realistic problems, they are looking for a specific answer with a small initial probability. In this case, positive tests are usually more informative than negative tests.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-klaymanha-20

However, in Wason's rule discovery task the answer—three numbers in ascending order—is very broad, so positive tests are unlikely to yield informative answers. Klayman and Ha supported their analysis by citing an experiment that used the labels "DAX" and "MED" in place of "fits the rule" and "doesn't fit the rule". This avoided implying that the aim was to find a low-probability rule. Participants had much more success with this version of the experiment.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-69

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-70

If the true rule (T) encompasses the current hypothesis (H), then positive tests (examining an H to see if it is T) will not show that the hypothesis is false.

If the true rule (T)

the current hypothesis (H), then either a negative test or a positive test can potentially falsify H.

When the working hypothesis (H) includes the true rule (T) then positive tests are the

way to falsify H.

In light of this and other critiques, the focus of research moved away from confirmation versus falsification of an hypothesis, to examining whether people test hypotheses in an informative way, or an uninformative but positive way. The search for "true" confirmation bias led psychologists to look at a wider range of effects in how people process information.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-71

Information processing explanations

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=11

There are currently three main

information processing

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information\_processing\_(psychology)

explanations of confirmation bias, plus a recent addition.

Cognitive versus motivational

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=12

Happy events are more likely to be remembered.

According to

Robert MacCoun

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert\_MacCoun

, most biased evidence processing occurs through a combination of "cold" (cognitive) and "hot" (motivated) mechanisms.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-72

Cognitive explanations for confirmation bias are based on limitations in people's ability to handle complex tasks, and the shortcuts, called

, that they use.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-73

For example, people may judge the reliability of evidence by using the

availability heuristic

that is, how readily a particular idea comes to mind.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-74

It is also possible that people can only focus on one thought at a time, so find it difficult to test alternative hypotheses in parallel.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Another heuristic is the positive test strategy identified by Klayman and Ha, in which people test a hypothesis by examining cases where they expect a property or event to occur. This heuristic avoids the difficult or impossible task of working out how diagnostic each possible question will be. However, it is not universally reliable, so people can overlook challenges to their existing beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-klaymanha-20

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Motivational explanations involve an effect of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desire\_(emotion)

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belief

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-75

It is known that people prefer positive thoughts over negative ones in a number of ways: this is called the "

Pollyanna principle

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollyanna\_principle

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-76

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argument

or sources of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evidence

, this could explain why desired conclusions are more likely to be believed true. According to experiments that manipulate the desirability of the conclusion, people demand a high standard of evidence for unpalatable ideas and a low standard for preferred ideas. In other words, they ask, "Can I believe this?" for some suggestions and, "Must I believe this?" for others.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-77

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-78

consistency

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consistency

is a desirable feature of attitudes, an excessive drive for consistency is another potential source of bias because it may prevent people from neutrally evaluating new, surprising information. Social psychologist

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziva\_Kunda

combines the cognitive and motivational theories, arguing that motivation creates the bias, but cognitive factors determine the size of the effect.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Cost-benefit

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=13

Explanations in terms of

cost-benefit analysis

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost-benefit\_analysis

assume that people do not just test hypotheses in a disinterested way, but assess the costs of different errors.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-79

Using ideas from

evolutionary psychology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary\_psychology

, James Friedrich suggests that people do not primarily aim at

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth

in testing hypotheses, but try to avoid the most costly errors. For example, employers might ask one-sided questions in job interviews because they are focused on weeding out unsuitable candidates.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-80

Yaacov Trope

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaacov\_Trope

and Akiva Liberman's refinement of this theory assumes that people compare the two different kinds of error: accepting a false hypothesis or rejecting a true hypothesis. For instance, someone who underestimates a friend's honesty might treat him or her suspiciously and so undermine the friendship. Overestimating the friend's honesty may also be costly, but less so. In this case, it would be rational to seek, evaluate or remember evidence of their honesty in a biased way.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-81

When someone gives an initial impression of being introverted or extroverted, questions that match that impression come across as more

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empathic

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-dardenne-82

This suggests that when talking to someone who seems to be an introvert, it is a sign of better

social skills

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social\_skills

to ask, "Do you feel awkward in social situations?" rather than, "Do you like noisy parties?" The connection between confirmation bias and social skills was corroborated by a study of how college students get to know other people. Highly

self-monitoring

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-monitoring

students, who are more sensitive to their environment and to

social norms

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social\_norms

, asked more matching questions when interviewing a high-status staff member than when getting to know fellow students.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-dardenne-82

Exploratory versus confirmatory

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=14

Psychologists

Jennifer Lerner

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer\_Lerner

Philip Tetlock

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip\_Tetlock

distinguish two different kinds of thinking process.

Exploratory thought

neutrally considers multiple points of view and tries to anticipate all possible objections to a particular position, while

confirmatory thought

seeks to justify a specific point of view. Lerner and Tetlock say that when people expect to justify their position to others whose views they already know, they will tend to adopt a similar position to those people, and then use confirmatory thought to bolster their own credibility. However, if the external parties are overly aggressive or critical, people will disengage from thought altogether, and simply assert their personal opinions without justification. Lerner and Tetlock say that people only push themselves to think critically and logically when they know in advance they will need to explain themselves to others who are well-informed, genuinely interested in the truth, and whose views they do not already know. Because those conditions rarely exist, they argue, most people are using confirmatory thought most of the time.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-83

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-84

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-85

Make-believe

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=15

Developmental psychologist Eve Whitmore has argued that beliefs and biases involved in confirmation bias have their roots in childhood coping through make-believe, which becomes "the basis for more complex forms of self-deception and illusion into adulthood." The friction brought on by questioning as an adolescent with developing critical thinking can lead to the rationalization of false beliefs, and the habit of such rationalization can become unconscious over the years.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-apa-86

Optimal information acquisition

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=16

Recent research in economics has challenged the traditional view of confirmation bias as purely a cognitive flaw.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-oi-87

Under conditions where acquiring and processing information is costly, seeking confirmatory evidence can actually be an optimal strategy. Instead of pursuing contrarian or disconfirming evidence, it may be more efficient to focus on sources likely to align with one's existing beliefs, given the constraints on time and resources.

Economist Weijie Zhong has developed a model demonstrating that individuals who must make decisions under time pressure, and who face costs for obtaining more information, will often prefer confirmatory signals. According to this model, when individuals believe strongly in a certain hypothesis, they optimally seek information that confirms it, allowing them to build confidence more efficiently. If the expected confirmatory signals are not received, their confidence in the initial hypothesis will gradually decline, leading to belief updating. This approach shows that seeking confirmation is not necessarily biased but may be a rational allocation of limited attention and resources.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-zhong-88

Real-world effects

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=17

Social media

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=18

social media

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social\_media

, confirmation bias is amplified by the use of

filter bubbles

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter\_bubble

echo chambers

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo\_chamber\_(media)

(or "algorithmic editing"), which displays to individuals only information they are likely to agree with, while excluding opposing views.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-:0-89

Some have argued that confirmation bias is the reason why society can never escape from filter bubbles, because individuals are psychologically hardwired to seek information that agrees with their preexisting values and beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-90

Others have further argued that the mixture of the two is degrading

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy

—claiming that this "algorithmic editing" removes diverse viewpoints and information—and that unless filter bubble algorithms are removed, voters will be unable to make fully informed political decisions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-91

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-:0-89

The rise of social media has contributed greatly to the rapid spread of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake\_news

, that is, false and misleading information that is presented as credible news from a seemingly reliable source. Confirmation bias (selecting or reinterpreting evidence to support one's beliefs) is one of three main hurdles cited as to why critical thinking goes astray in these circumstances. The other two are shortcut heuristics (when overwhelmed or short of time, people rely on simple rules such as group consensus or trusting an expert or role model) and social goals (social motivation or peer pressure can interfere with objective analysis of facts at hand).

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-92

In combating the spread of fake news, social media sites have considered turning toward "digital nudging".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-93

This can currently be done in two different forms of nudging. This includes nudging of information and nudging of presentation. Nudging of information entails social media sites providing a disclaimer or label questioning or warning users of the validity of the source while nudging of presentation includes exposing users to new information which they may not have sought out but could introduce them to viewpoints that may combat their own confirmation biases.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-94

Science and scientific research

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=19

Planck's principle

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck's\_principle

Escalation of commitment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escalation\_of\_commitment

Replication crisis

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replication\_crisis

A distinguishing feature of

scientific thinking

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science

is the search for confirming or supportive evidence (

inductive reasoning

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive\_reasoning

) as well as falsifying evidence (

deductive reasoning

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive\_reasoning

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-95

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-96

Many times in the

history of science

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History\_of\_science

, scientists have resisted new discoveries by selectively interpreting or ignoring unfavorable data.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Several studies have shown that scientists rate studies that report findings consistent with their prior beliefs more favorably than studies reporting findings inconsistent with their previous beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Hergovich\_2010-13

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Koehler\_1993-97

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Mahoney\_1977-98

However, assuming that the research question is relevant, the experimental design adequate and the data are clearly and comprehensively described, the empirical data obtained should be important to the scientific community and should not be viewed prejudicially, regardless of whether they conform to current theoretical predictions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Mahoney\_1977-98

In practice, researchers may misunderstand, misinterpret, or not read at all studies that contradict their preconceptions, or wrongly cite them anyway as if they actually supported their claims.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Letrud\_&\_Hernes\_2019-99

Further, confirmation biases can sustain scientific theories or research programs in the face of inadequate or even contradictory evidence.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-sutherland-65

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-100

The discipline of

parapsychology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parapsychology

is often cited as an example.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-101

An experimenter's confirmation bias can potentially affect which data are reported. Data that conflict with the experimenter's expectations may be more readily discarded as unreliable, producing the so-called

file drawer effect

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publication\_bias

. To combat this tendency, scientific training teaches ways to prevent bias.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shadish-102

For example,

experimental design

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Design\_of\_experiments

randomized controlled trials

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randomized\_controlled\_trial

(coupled with their

systematic review

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systematic\_review

) aims to minimize sources of bias.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shadish-102

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-103

The social process of

peer review

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer\_review

aims to mitigate the effect of individual scientists' biases, even though the peer review process itself may be susceptible to such biases.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-104

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-105

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Mahoney\_1977-98

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-106

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Bartlett\_2011-107

Confirmation bias may thus be especially harmful to objective evaluations regarding nonconforming results since biased individuals may regard opposing evidence to be weak in principle and give little serious thought to revising their beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-Koehler\_1993-97

Scientific innovators often meet with resistance from the scientific community, and research presenting controversial results frequently receives harsh peer review.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-108

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=20

Escalation of commitment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escalation\_of\_commitment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunk\_cost

Confirmation bias can lead investors to be overconfident, ignoring evidence that their strategies will lose money.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-WSJ-14

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-109

In studies of

political stock markets

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election\_stock\_market

, investors made more profit when they resisted bias. For example, participants who interpreted a candidate's debate performance in a neutral rather than partisan way were more likely to profit.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-110

To combat the effect of confirmation bias, investors can try to adopt a contrary viewpoint "for the sake of argument".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-111

In one technique, they imagine that their investments have collapsed and ask themselves why this might happen.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-WSJ-14

Medicine and health

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=21

Cognitive biases are important variables in clinical decision-making by medical general practitioners (GPs) and medical specialists. Two important ones are confirmation bias and the overlapping availability bias. A GP may make a diagnosis early on during an examination, and then seek confirming evidence rather than falsifying evidence. This cognitive error is partly caused by the availability of evidence about the supposed disorder being diagnosed. For example, the client may have mentioned the disorder, or the GP may have recently read a much-discussed paper about the disorder. The basis of this cognitive shortcut or heuristic (termed anchoring) is that the doctor does not consider multiple possibilities based on evidence, but prematurely latches on (or anchors to) a single cause.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-112

In emergency medicine, because of time pressure, there is a high density of decision-making, and shortcuts are frequently applied. The potential failure rate of these cognitive decisions needs to be managed by education about the 30 or more cognitive biases that can occur, so as to set in place proper debiasing strategies.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-croskerry-113

Confirmation bias may also cause doctors to perform unnecessary medical procedures due to pressure from adamant patients.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-hospitalbias-114

Mental disorders may be prone to misdiagnosis in being based upon observations and self-reporting rather than objective testing. Confirmation bias may play a role when practitioners stick with an early diagnosis.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-115

Raymond Nickerson, a psychologist, blames confirmation bias for the ineffective medical procedures that were used for centuries before the

arrival of scientific medicine

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History\_of\_medicine

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

If a patient recovered, medical authorities counted the treatment as successful, rather than looking for alternative explanations such as that the disease had run its natural course. Biased assimilation is a factor in the modern appeal of

alternative medicine

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative\_medicine

, whose proponents are swayed by positive

anecdotal evidence

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anecdotal\_evidence

scientific evidence

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific\_evidence

hyper-critically.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-116

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-117

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-118

Cognitive therapy

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_therapy

was developed by

Aaron T. Beck

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron\_T.\_Beck

in the early 1960s and has become a popular approach.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-119

According to Beck, biased information processing is a factor in

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression\_(mood)

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-120

His approach teaches people to treat evidence impartially, rather than selectively reinforcing negative outlooks.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron195-54

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phobias

hypochondria

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypochondria

have also been shown to involve confirmation bias for threatening information.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-121

Politics, law and policing

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=22

Mock trials

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mock\_trial

allow researchers to examine confirmation biases in a realistic setting.

Nickerson argues that reasoning in judicial and political contexts is sometimes subconsciously biased, favoring conclusions that judges, juries or governments have already committed to.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Since the evidence in a jury trial can be complex, and jurors often reach decisions about the verdict early on, it is reasonable to expect an attitude polarization effect. The prediction that jurors will become more extreme in their views as they see more evidence has been borne out in experiments with

mock trials

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mock\_trial

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-122

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-halpern-123

inquisitorial

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisitorial\_system

adversarial

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adversarial\_system

criminal justice systems are affected by confirmation bias.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-124

Confirmation bias can be a factor in creating or extending conflicts, from emotionally charged debates to wars: by interpreting the evidence in their favor, each opposing party can become overconfident that it is in the stronger position.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron191-125

On the other hand, confirmation bias can result in people ignoring or misinterpreting the signs of an imminent or incipient conflict. For example, psychologists

Stuart Sutherland

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart\_Sutherland

and Thomas Kida have each argued that

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.\_Navy

Husband E. Kimmel

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husband\_E.\_Kimmel

showed confirmation bias when playing down the first signs of the Japanese

attack on Pearl Harbor

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack\_on\_Pearl\_Harbor

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-sutherland-65

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-126

A two-decade study of political pundits by

Philip E. Tetlock

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip\_E.\_Tetlock

found that, on the whole, their predictions were not much better than chance. Tetlock divided experts into "foxes" who maintained multiple hypotheses, and "hedgehogs" who were more dogmatic. In general, the hedgehogs were much less accurate. Tetlock blamed their failure on confirmation bias, and specifically on their inability to make use of new information that contradicted their existing theories.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-127

In police investigations, a detective may identify a suspect early in an investigation, but then sometimes largely seek supporting or confirming evidence, ignoring or downplaying falsifying evidence.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-128

Self concept and self esteem

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=23

Social psychologists have identified two tendencies in the way people seek or interpret information about themselves.

Self-verification

is the drive to reinforce the existing

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-image

self-enhancement

is the drive to seek positive feedback. Both are served by confirmation biases.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-reconciling-129

In experiments where people are given feedback that conflicts with their self-image, they are less likely to attend to it or remember it than when given self-verifying feedback.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-swannread\_jesp-130

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-131

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-132

They reduce the impact of such information by interpreting it as unreliable.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-swannread\_jesp-130

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-swannread\_jpsp-133

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-134

Similar experiments have found a preference for positive feedback, and the people who give it, over negative feedback.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-reconciling-129

Mass delusions

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=24

Confirmation bias can play a key role in the propagation of

mass delusions

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass\_delusion

Witch trials

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch\_trial

are frequently cited as an example.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-135

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-136

For another example, in the

Seattle windshield pitting epidemic

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle\_windshield\_pitting\_epidemic

, there seemed to be a "pitting epidemic" in which windshields were damaged due to an unknown cause. As news of the apparent wave of damage spread, more and more people checked their windshields, discovered that their windshields too had been damaged, thus confirming belief in the supposed epidemic. In fact, the windshields were previously damaged, but the damage went unnoticed until people checked their windshields as the delusion spread.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-137

Paranormal beliefs

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=25

One factor in the appeal of alleged

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychic

readings is that listeners apply a confirmation bias which fits the psychic's statements to their own lives.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-toolkit-138

By making a large number of ambiguous statements in each sitting, the psychic gives the client more opportunities to find a match. This is one of the techniques of

cold reading

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold\_reading

, with which a psychic can deliver a subjectively impressive reading without any prior information about the client.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-toolkit-138

Investigator

James Randi

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James\_Randi

compared the transcript of a reading to the client's report of what the psychic had said, and found that the client showed a strong selective recall of the "hits".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-139

As a striking illustration of confirmation bias in the real world, Nickerson mentions numerological

pyramidology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramidology

: the practice of finding meaning in the proportions of the Egyptian pyramids.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

There are many different length measurements that can be made of, for example, the

Great Pyramid of Giza

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great\_Pyramid\_of\_Giza

and many ways to combine or manipulate them. Hence it is almost inevitable that people who look at these numbers selectively will find superficially impressive correspondences, for example with the dimensions of the Earth.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Recruitment and selection

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=26

Unconscious cognitive bias (including confirmation bias) in

job recruitment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recruitment

can affect hiring decisions and can potentially prohibit a diverse and inclusive workplace. There are a variety of unconscious biases that can affect recruitment decisions, and confirmation bias is one of the major ones, especially during the interview stage.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-140

The interviewer may select a candidate that confirms their own beliefs, even though other candidates are equally or better qualified.

Associated effects and outcomes

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=27

Polarization of opinion

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=28

#### Main article:

Attitude polarization

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attitude\_polarization

When people with opposing views interpret new information in a biased way, their views can move even further apart. This is called "attitude polarization".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kuhn\_lao-141

The effect was demonstrated by an experiment that involved drawing a series of red and black balls from one of two concealed "bingo baskets". Participants knew that one basket contained 60 percent black and 40 percent red balls; the other, 40 percent black and 60 percent red. The experimenters looked at what happened when balls of alternating color were drawn in turn, a sequence that does not favor either basket. After each ball was drawn, participants in one group were asked to state out loud their judgments of the probability that the balls were being drawn from one or the other basket. These participants tended to grow more confident with each successive draw—whether they initially thought the basket with 60 percent black balls or the one with 60 percent red balls was the more likely source, their estimate of the probability increased. Another group of participants were asked to state probability estimates only at the end of a sequence of drawn balls, rather than after each ball. They did not show the polarization effect, suggesting that it does not necessarily occur when people simply hold opposing positions, but rather when they openly commit to them.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-142

A less abstract study was the Stanford biased interpretation experiment, in which participants with strong opinions about the death penalty read about mixed experimental evidence. Twenty-three percent of the participants reported that their views had become more extreme, and this self-reported shift correlated strongly with their initial attitudes.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-lord1979-31

In later experiments, participants also reported their opinions becoming more extreme in response to ambiguous information. However, comparisons of their attitudes before and after the new evidence showed no significant change, suggesting that the self-reported changes might not be real.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-taber\_political-34

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kuhn\_lao-141

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-143

Based on these experiments, Deanna Kuhn and Joseph Lao concluded that polarization is a real phenomenon but far from inevitable, only happening in a small minority of cases, and it was prompted not only by considering mixed evidence, but by merely thinking about the topic.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kuhn\_lao-141

Charles Taber and Milton Lodge argued that the Stanford team's result had been hard to replicate because the arguments used in later experiments were too abstract or confusing to evoke an emotional response. The Taber and Lodge study used the emotionally charged topics of

gun control

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun\_politics

affirmative action

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative\_action

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-taber\_political-34

They measured the attitudes of their participants towards these issues before and after reading arguments on each side of the debate. Two groups of participants showed attitude polarization: those with strong prior opinions and those who were politically knowledgeable. In part of this study, participants chose which information sources to read, from a list prepared by the experimenters. For example, they could read arguments on gun control from the

National Rifle Association of America

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National\_Rifle\_Association\_of\_America

Brady Anti-Handgun Coalition

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brady\_Campaign

. Even when instructed to be even-handed, participants were more likely to read arguments that supported their existing attitudes than arguments that did not. This biased search for information correlated well with the polarization effect.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-taber\_political-34

backfire effect

is a name for the finding that given evidence against their beliefs, people can reject the evidence and believe even more strongly.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-144

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-CJR\_backfire-145

The phrase was coined by

Brendan Nyhan

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan\_Nyhan

and Jason Reifler in 2010.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-146

However, subsequent research has since failed to replicate findings supporting the backfire effect.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-147

One study conducted out of the Ohio State University and George Washington University studied 10,100 participants with 52 different issues expected to trigger a backfire effect. While the findings did conclude that individuals are reluctant to embrace facts that contradict their already held ideology, no cases of backfire were detected.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-wood-148

The backfire effect has since been noted to be a rare phenomenon rather than a common occurrence

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-149

(compare the

boomerang effect

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boomerang\_effect\_(psychology)

Persistence of discredited beliefs

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=29

#### Main article:

Belief perseverance

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belief\_perseverance

Cognitive dissonance

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_dissonance

Monty Hall problem

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monty\_Hall\_problem

Beliefs can survive potent logical or empirical challenges. They can survive and even be bolstered by evidence that most uncommitted observers would agree logically demands some weakening of such beliefs. They can even survive the total destruction of their original evidential bases.

— Lee Ross and Craig Anderson

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shortcomings-150

Confirmation biases provide one plausible explanation for the persistence of beliefs when the initial evidence for them is removed or when they have been sharply contradicted.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

This belief perseverance effect has been first demonstrated experimentally by

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon\_Festinger

, Riecken, and Schachter. These psychologists

spent time with

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participant\_observation

a cult whose members were convinced that the world would end on 21 December 1954. After the prediction failed, most believers still clung to their faith. Their book describing this research is aptly named

When Prophecy Fails

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-151

belief perseverance

, however, was coined in a series of experiments using what is called the "debriefing paradigm": participants read fake evidence for a hypothesis, their

attitude change

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attitude\_change

is measured, then the fakery is exposed in detail. Their attitudes are then measured once more to see if their belief returns to its previous level.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shortcomings-150

A common finding is that at least some of the initial belief remains even after a full debriefing.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kunda99-152

In one experiment, participants had to distinguish between real and fake suicide notes. The feedback was random: some were told they had done well while others were told they had performed badly. Even after being fully debriefed, participants were still influenced by the feedback. They still thought they were better or worse than average at that kind of task, depending on what they had initially been told.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-153

In another study, participants read

job performance

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job\_performance

ratings of two firefighters, along with their responses to a

risk aversion

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risk\_aversion

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shortcomings-150

This fictional data was arranged to show either a negative or positive association: some participants were told that a risk-taking firefighter did better, while others were told they did less well than a risk-averse colleague.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-socialperseverance-154

Even if these two case studies were true, they would have been scientifically poor evidence for a conclusion about firefighters in general. However, the participants found them subjectively persuasive.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-socialperseverance-154

When the case studies were shown to be fictional, participants' belief in a link diminished, but around half of the original effect remained.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-shortcomings-150

Follow-up interviews established that the participants had understood the debriefing and taken it seriously. Participants seemed to trust the debriefing, but regarded the discredited information as irrelevant to their personal belief.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-socialperseverance-154

continued influence effect

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continued\_influence\_effect

is the tendency for misinformation to continue to influence memory and reasoning about an event, despite the misinformation having been retracted or corrected. This occurs even when the individual believes the correction.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-155

Preference for early information

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=30

Experiments have shown that information is weighted more strongly when it appears early in a series, even when the order is unimportant. For example, people form a more positive impression of someone described as "intelligent, industrious, impulsive, critical, stubborn, envious" than when they are given the same words in reverse order.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron197-156

irrational primacy effect

is independent of the

primacy effect in memory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serial\_position\_effect

in which the earlier items in a series leave a stronger memory trace.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron197-156

Biased interpretation offers an explanation for this effect: seeing the initial evidence, people form a working hypothesis that affects how they interpret the rest of the information.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

One demonstration of irrational primacy used colored chips supposedly drawn from two urns. Participants were told the color distributions of the urns, and had to estimate the probability of a chip being drawn from one of them.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron197-156

In fact, the colors appeared in a prearranged order. The first thirty draws favored one urn and the next thirty favored the other.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

The series as a whole was neutral, so rationally, the two urns were equally likely. However, after sixty draws, participants favored the urn suggested by the initial thirty.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron197-156

Another experiment involved a slide show of a single object, seen as just a blur at first and in slightly better focus with each succeeding slide.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-baron197-156

After each slide, participants had to state their best guess of what the object was. Participants whose early guesses were wrong persisted with those guesses, even when the picture was sufficiently in focus that the object was readily recognizable to other people.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-nickerson-4

Illusory association between events

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=31

#### Main article:

Illusory correlation

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illusory\_correlation

Illusory correlation is the tendency to see non-existent correlations in a set of data.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-fine-157

This tendency was first demonstrated in a series of experiments in the late 1960s.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-plous164-158

In one experiment, participants read a set of psychiatric case studies, including responses to the

Rorschach inkblot test

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rorschach\_inkblot\_test

. The participants reported that the homosexual men in the set were more likely to report seeing buttocks, anuses or sexually ambiguous figures in the inkblots. In fact the fictional case studies had been constructed so that the homosexual men were no more likely to report this imagery or, in one version of the experiment, were less likely to report it than heterosexual men.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-fine-157

In a survey, a group of experienced psychoanalysts reported the same set of illusory associations with homosexuality.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-fine-157

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-plous164-158

Another study recorded the symptoms experienced by arthritic patients, along with weather conditions over a 15-month period. Nearly all the patients reported that their pains were correlated with weather conditions, although the real correlation was zero.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-159

No arthritis

This effect is a kind of biased interpretation, in that objectively neutral or unfavorable evidence is interpreted to support existing beliefs. It is also related to biases in hypothesis-testing behavior.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kunda127-160

In judging whether two events, such as illness and bad weather, are correlated, people rely heavily on the number of

positive-positive

cases: in this example, instances of both pain and bad weather. They pay relatively little attention to the other kinds of observation (of no pain or good weather).

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-plous162-161

This parallels the reliance on positive tests in hypothesis testing.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kunda127-160

It may also reflect selective recall, in that people may have a sense that two events are correlated because it is easier to recall times when they happened together.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-kunda127-160

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Confirmation\_bias&action=edit&section=32

David Perkins

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David\_Perkins\_(geneticist)

, a professor and researcher at the Harvard Graduate School of Education, coined the term "myside bias" referring to a preference for "my" side of an issue.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-FOOTNOTEBaron2000195-1

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_ref-11

"Assimilation bias" is another term used for biased interpretation of evidence.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_note-FOOTNOTERisenGilovich2007113-10

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation\_bias#cite\_ref-62

Wason also used the term "verification bias".
